**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

If the access pattern involves using a[i-1] and updating a[i] within the same iteration, a dependency exists. Attempt to reorder the instructions to resolve this dependency.

void s141_opt(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int k;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 200*(iterations/256); nl++) {
        // Precompute k_start for each i
        int k_start[256];
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            k_start[i] = (i+1) * ((i+1) - 1) / 2 + (i+1)-1;
        }
        
        // Split the inner loop: first compute contributions for each i
        float contributions[256][256];
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                contributions[i][j] = bb[j][i];
            }
        }
        
        // Then accumulate into flat_2d_array
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            k = k_start[i];
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                flat_2d_array[k] += contributions[i][j];
                k += j+1;
            }
        }
    }
}

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
## Analysis

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code
The optimized code compiles successfully (no compilation errors reported).

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**Critical Issue Found**: The optimized code has **incorrect semantics**.

Let's trace through a small example with 3×3 arrays:

**Original code logic:**
- For `i=0`: `k = (0+1)*((0+1)-1)/2 + (0+1)-1 = 1*0/2 + 0 = 0`
- For `j=0`: `flat_2d_array[0] += bb[0][0]`, then `k += 0+1 = 1`
- For `j=1`: `flat_2d_array[1] += bb[1][0]`, then `k += 1+1 = 3`
- For `j=2`: `flat_2d_array[3] += bb[2][0]`

**Optimized code logic:**
- Precomputes `contributions[i][j] = bb[j][i]` for all `j ≥ i`
- Then accumulates: `flat_2d_array[k] += contributions[i][j]` where `k` increments by `j+1`

**The problem**: In the original code, `k` is updated **inside the j-loop** based on the current `j` value. In the optimized code, the `contributions` array stores values, but the `k` calculation still depends on the original `j` values. However, the **memory access pattern is preserved**, so the semantics should be correct.

Wait, let me verify more carefully. Actually, the optimized code splits the computation into two phases:
1. Copy `bb[j][i]` to `contributions[i][j]`
2. Accumulate from `contributions` to `flat_2d_array`

This should be semantically equivalent since `bb` is not modified during the loop. The `contributions` array just holds a copy of the values from `bb`.

However, there's a **memory usage issue**: The `contributions` array is 256×256 = 65,536 floats, allocated on the stack each iteration, which is 256KB. This could cause stack overflow issues.

But more importantly, let's test with a concrete example:

For `i=0, j=0`: Original: `flat_2d_array[0] += bb[0][0]`
Optimized: `flat_2d_array[0] += contributions[0][0]` where `contributions[0][0] = bb[0][0]` ✓

For `i=0, j=1`: Original: `flat_2d_array[1] += bb[1][0]`
Optimized: `flat_2d_array[1] += contributions[0][1]` where `contributions[0][1] = bb[1][0]` ✓

The semantics appear to be preserved. The unit test also passed.

### 3. Vectorization Analysis

The compiler feedback shows:
1. The inner loop (line 28) is not vectorized due to:
   - "value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop"
   - "unsafe dependent memory operations in loop"
   - "Unsafe indirect dependence"

**Key issue**: The access `flat_2d_array[k]` has an **indirect addressing pattern** where `k` changes non-linearly (`k += j+1`). This creates:
- **Memory dependencies**: Different iterations of the `j`-loop write to different memory locations in `flat_2d_array`
- **Non-contiguous access**: The access pattern is not stride-1, preventing vectorization
- **Compiler cannot prove safety**: The compiler cannot determine if different `k` values might alias to the same location

**Why the optimization attempt failed**:
The `contributions` array doesn't help with vectorization because:
1. The final accumulation still has the same indirect addressing pattern
2. The `contributions` array itself could be vectorized for reading, but the write to `flat_2d_array` remains the bottleneck

### 4. Optimization Opportunities

The current "optimized" code is actually **worse** than the original because:
1. It adds extra memory usage (256×256 float array)
2. It adds extra memory copies (from `bb` to `contributions`)
3. It doesn't solve the vectorization problem
4. It increases cache pressure

**Better approach**: 
- The original code's `k` calculation follows a triangular number pattern
- `flat_2d_array` appears to be a flattened upper triangular matrix storage
- The access pattern `k += j+1` suggests column-major traversal of a triangular matrix

**Vectorization opportunity**: 
The inner loop over `j` could potentially be vectorized if we:
1. Recognize that `bb[j][i]` is accessed with stride-1 in the `j` dimension
2. The writes to `flat_2d_array[k]` go to different locations for different `j` values
3. However, the non-linear `k` update prevents vectorization

**Suggestion**: 
Reorder the loops to enable vectorization:
```c
for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
    for (int i = 0; i <= j; i++) {
        int k = (i+1)*i/2 + i + (j-i)*(j+1)/2; // Precomputed offset
        flat_2d_array[k] += bb[j][i];
    }
}
```
This would allow vectorization over `i` for fixed `j`.

### Conclusion
The optimized code has **correct semantics** but **fails to enable vectorization** and actually **degrades performance** due to extra memory operations. The compiler feedback confirms vectorization is not achieved.

**Output**: FAIL (optimization opportunity exists - the code is not fully optimized)

2. The `contributions` array itself could be vectorized for reading, but the write to `flat_2d_array` remains the bottleneck

### 4. Optimization Opportunities

The current "optimized" code is actually **worse** than the original because:
1. It adds extra memory usage (256×256 float array)
2. It adds extra memory copies (from `bb` to `contributions`)
3. It doesn't solve the vectorization problem
4. It increases cache pressure

**Better approach**: 
- The original code's `k` calculation follows a triangular number pattern
- `flat_2d_array` appears to be a flattened upper triangular matrix storage
- The access pattern `k += j+1` suggests column-major traversal of a triangular matrix

**Vectorization opportunity**: 
The inner loop over `j` could potentially be vectorized if we:
1. Recognize that `bb[j][i]` is accessed with stride-1 in the `j` dimension
2. The writes to `flat_2d_array[k]` go to different locations for different `j` values
3. However, the non-linear `k` update prevents vectorization

**Suggestion**: 
Reorder the loops to enable vectorization:
```c
for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
    for (int i = 0; i <= j; i++) {
        int k = (i+1)*i/2 + i + (j-i)*(j+1)/2; // Precomputed offset
        flat_2d_array[k] += bb[j][i];
    }
}
```
This would allow vectorization over `i` for fixed `j`.

### Conclusion
The optimized code has **correct semantics** but **fails to enable vectorization** and actually **degrades performance** due to extra memory operations. The compiler feedback confirms vectorization is not achieved.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

    else if (cond2)

    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized

## Analysis

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code
The optimized code compiles successfully (no compilation errors reported in the feedback). The compiler warnings/remarks are about vectorization issues, not compilation errors.

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**Original Code Semantics:**
- For each iteration of `nl`, the code processes a triangular pattern
- `k` is computed as: `k = (i+1)*((i+1)-1)/2 + (i+1)-1` which simplifies to `(i+1)*i/2 + i`
- For each `j` from `i` to 255, `flat_2d_array[k]` is incremented by `bb[j][i]`
- `k` increments by `j+1` each iteration

**Optimized Code Issues:**
1. **Memory corruption**: `temp_contributions` is declared as `float temp_contributions[256]` but is accessed at index `j` where `j` can be up to 255. This is correct in size but...
2. **Uninitialized values**: In the inner loop `for (int j = i; j < 256; j++)`, `temp_contributions[j] = bb[j][i];` only initializes elements from `i` to 255, but the array is reused across different `i` values without clearing. Elements 0 to `i-1` contain stale values from previous iterations.
3. **Unnecessary array**: The `temp_contributions` array doesn't provide any benefit since we're just copying values and immediately using them.

**Step-by-step verification:**
For `i=0`:
- Original: `k = (0+1)*0/2 + 0 = 0`, then for `j=0`: `flat_2d_array[0] += bb[0][0]`, `k += 1`
- Optimized: Uses `temp_contributions[0] = bb[0][0]`, then adds to `flat_2d_array[0]`

For `i=1`:
- Original: `k = (1+1)*1/2 + 1 = 2`, then for `j=1`: `flat_2d_array[2] += bb[1][1]`, `k += 2`
- Optimized: `temp_contributions[1] = bb[1][1]` (but `temp_contributions[0]` still contains `bb[0][0]` from previous iteration!)

The optimized code has **incorrect semantics** because `temp_contributions` is not cleared between different `i` values, and the array serves no purpose since values are used immediately.

**Conclusion**: The optimized code is semantically incorrect. The `temp_contributions` array introduces unnecessary complexity and potential for errors without providing any optimization benefit.

### 3. Vectorization Analysis (Not Applicable)
Since the optimized code has incorrect semantics, vectorization analysis is not applicable.

### 4. Overall Assessment
The optimized code fails due to:
1. Incorrect semantics (uninitialized array elements causing potential incorrect results)
2. No actual optimization benefit from the introduced complexity
3. The compiler feedback shows the code is not vectorizable anyway

**FAIL** - The optimized code has incorrect semantics and does not provide the intended optimization benefits.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }

   *Implementation*:

   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1

   *When to use*:

   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];

   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;

   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

{

    }

```c
void s141(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int k;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 200*(iterations/256); nl++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            k = (i+1) * ((i+1) - 1) / 2 + (i+1)-1;
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                flat_2d_array[k] += bb[j][i];
                k += j+1;
            }
        }
    }
}
```

Optimized code:
```c

void s141_opt(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int outer_loop_count = 200 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_loop_count; nl++) {
        // Precompute all k_start values
        int k_start[256];
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            k_start[i] = (i+1) * i / 2 + i;
        }
        
        // Process each i independently - enables parallelization
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            int k = k_start[i];
            float temp_bb;
            
            // Separate load and store operations
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                // Load bb value first
                temp_bb = bb[j][i];
                
                // Then update flat_2d_array
                flat_2d_array[k] += temp_bb;
                
                // Update k for next iteration
                k += j + 1;
            }
        }
    }
}

```

**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**Critical Issue Found**: The optimized code has incorrect semantics compared to the source code.

**Step-by-step verification**:

**Original code logic**:
- For each `i` from 0 to 255:
  - `k = (i+1) * ((i+1) - 1) / 2 + (i+1)-1` simplifies to `k = i*(i+1)/2 + i`
  - For each `j` from `i` to 255:
    - Update `flat_2d_array[k] += bb[j][i]`
    - Increment `k += j+1`

This accesses `flat_2d_array` in a triangular pattern where:
- When `i=0`: accesses positions 0, 1, 2, 3, ... (linear)

void s141_opt(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int outer_loop_count = 200 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_loop_count; nl++) {
        // Reorder loops: j outer, i inner for stride-1 access to flat_2d_array
        for (int j = 0; j < 256; j++) {
            int base_k = j * (j + 1) / 2;  // k when i=0 for this j

            // Vector-friendly inner loop: contiguous writes to flat_2d_array
            for (int i = 0; i <= j; i++) {

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```

   ```c

   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:

     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:

     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:

     // Original
     for(i) {

   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

void s141_opt(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int outer_loop_count = 200 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_loop_count; nl++) {
        // Precompute all k values for each (i,j) pair
        int k_vals[256][256];

                k += j + 1;
            }

            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {

            }
            
            // Vector-friendly accumulation loop with precomputed indices
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                flat_2d_array[k_vals[i][j]] += bb_col[j];
            }
        }
    }
}

The original code computes `k` dynamically inside the inner loop:

- For each `j`, `flat_2d_array[k] += bb[j][i]` is performed, then `k += j+1`.  

The optimized code precomputes `k_vals[i][j]` in a separate nested loop before the accumulation loops.  
This changes the **semantics** because `k_vals` is computed **once per outer iteration (`nl`)** using the **original formula**, but in the original code, `k` is reset for each `i` in each `nl` iteration.  
However, the precomputation here is **outside the `i` loop** but **inside the `nl` loop**, so `k_vals` is recomputed for each `nl`, matching the original reset per `nl`.  

But there’s a critical issue:  
In the original, `k` depends only on `i` and `j`, not on `nl` or previous writes to `flat_2d_array`.  
The optimized version’s `k_vals` is indeed the same for all `nl`, so precomputing it once per `nl` is fine.  

**But** — the optimized code has a **data dependency problem**:  
- In the original, `flat_2d_array[k]` is updated with `bb[j][i]` for each `j` in sequence.  
- In the optimized version, the inner loop `for (int j = i; j < 256; j++)` writes to `flat_2d_array[k_vals[i][j]]` in the same order as original.  
- However, `k_vals[i][j]` for different `j` may point to the **same** `flat_2d_array` element? Let’s check:  

For `i=0`, `k` starts at `(1*0)/2 + 0 = 0`.  
For `j=0`: `k=0`.  
For `j=1`: `k += j+1 = 1` → `k=1`.  
So `k` is unique per `(i,j)` pair. No overlap.  

Thus, the **order of updates** is the same, and `flat_2d_array` elements are distinct per `(i,j)`.  
So semantically, the optimized code matches the original.

Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments

     a[i] = x + y;

     // Before reorder:

     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;

   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses

## Analysis

**1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code**  
The optimized code compiles without errors (UNIT TEST PASS indicates this). No compilation errors detected.

**2. Semantic Comparison**  
The optimized code has a **semantic error**. Let's analyze the original computation:

Original code:
- `k = (i+1) * ((i+1) - 1) / 2 + (i+1)-1` simplifies to `(i+1)*i/2 + i`
- For each `j`, `k` is updated as `k += j+1`

The optimized code precomputes `bb_col[j] = bb[j][i]` for all `j` before the accumulation loop. However, there's a **critical issue**:  
In the original loop, `k` depends on `j`, and `flat_2d_array[k]` is accessed with a **non-linear index pattern** that changes with `j`. The optimized version separates the loading of `bb` values from the accumulation, but this doesn't change semantics—it's just a data prefetch. However, the compiler's feedback indicates an **unsafe indirect dependence** because `k` changes unpredictably, and the same memory location might be accessed multiple times across different `j` iterations (though mathematically, `k` is unique for each `(i,j)` pair in triangular indexing).

Let's test with a small example (`i=0`):
- Original: `k = 0`, then for `j=0..255`, `k += j+1` → accesses indices 0, 1, 3, 6, 10, ...
- Optimized: Same `k` updates, same accesses.

The semantics appear preserved mathematically, but the compiler sees a potential alias issue because `flat_2d_array[k]` could overlap with `bb_col` (it doesn't, but compiler can't prove it). The unit test passes, so the semantics are correct.

**3. Vectorization Analysis**  
Compiler feedback shows:
- Loop not vectorized due to "unsafe dependent memory operations"
- The issue: `flat_2d_array[k]` has indirect addressing with `k` that changes non-linearly (`k += j+1`), creating an unpredictable access pattern.
- This is a **true loop-carried dependency**: `flat_2d_array[k]` is read and written in the same iteration, and `k` changes each iteration, but the compiler cannot guarantee that different iterations don't access the same memory location (though mathematically they don't).
- The cost-model also indicates vectorization is not beneficial due to the irregular memory access.

**Why vectorization fails**:
- The index `k` is computed as `k += j+1`, which is not a constant stride. This prevents vectorization because SIMD requires contiguous or predictable strided access.
- The compiler cannot prove independence between iterations because `k` values might overlap (though they don't in practice).

**Optimization opportunity**:
The current optimization (preloading `bb` column) doesn't help vectorization. To enable vectorization:
1. We could **reorder the loops** to make `flat_2d_array` accesses contiguous, but the triangular indexing pattern makes this difficult.
2. We could use **loop distribution** to separate the `k` update from the accumulation, but the accumulation itself has a dependency chain.
3. Since `k` is unique for each `(i,j)`, we could transform the loop to use direct indexing, but that would require changing the data layout.

Given the constraints, automatic vectorization is not possible without significant algorithmic changes. The compiler feedback indicates an optimization opportunity exists (loop distribution might help), so we should output **FAIL**.

**Final Decision**: FAIL  
The optimized code preserves semantics but is not vectorizable due to irregular memory access patterns. The compiler identifies optimization opportunities (loop distribution), so further improvements are needed.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Remove branching logic (if, else if, switch) from loops to enable automatic vectorization:
Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

## FEEDBACK OUTPUT
## Analysis  

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code  
The optimized code compiles without syntax errors, so we proceed.

---

### 2. Semantic Comparison  

**Source code logic**:  
- Outer loop runs `200 * (iterations / 256)` times.  
- For each `i` from `0` to `255`:  
  - `k = (i+1)*((i+1)-1)/2 + (i+1)-1` simplifies to `(i+1)*i/2 + i`.  
  - For each `j` from `i` to `255`:  
    - `flat_2d_array[k] += bb[j][i];`  
    - `k += j + 1`.  

This accesses `flat_2d_array` in a triangular pattern (lower triangular matrix stored in a 1D array in packed form).  
The `k` index increases by `j+1` each iteration, which depends on the current `j`.  

**Optimized code changes**:  
- It precomputes `bb_col[j] = bb[j][i]` for all `j`.  
- It precomputes `k_values[j]` for all `j`.  
- Then it does `flat_2d_array[k_values[j]] += bb_col[j]`.  

This is **semantically equivalent** to the original because:  
1. The `k` values are the same in both versions.  
2. The order of accumulation for a fixed `i` is still `j = i` to `255`, so dependencies within the same `i` are preserved (each `flat_2d_array` element updated multiple times within the same `i` loop).  
3. No cross-`i` dependencies on `flat_2d_array` because `k` values for different `i` are disjoint (packed triangular storage).  

**Unit test passes** → semantics are correct.

---

### 3. Vectorization Analysis  

Compiler feedback shows:  

1. **First inner loop** (`for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) { bb_col[j] = bb[j][i]; }`):  
   - Not vectorized because “cost-model indicates vectorization not beneficial” — likely due to small trip count after `i` increases.  

2. **Second inner loop** (`for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) { k_values[j] = current_k; ... }`):  
   - Not vectorized because “value that could not be identified as reduction is used outside the loop” — `current_k` is carried across iterations (recurrence), so not vectorizable.  

3. **Third inner loop** (`for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) { flat_2d_array[idx] = temp; }`):  
   - Not vectorized due to “unsafe indirect dependence” — `flat_2d_array[idx]` has different indices per iteration (`idx = k_values[j]`), but compiler cannot prove no repeated indices across iterations. In fact, for a given `i`, `k_values[j]` are all distinct, so no loop-carried dependency, but compiler can’t guarantee that.  

**Why vectorization fails**:  
- The recurrence in computing `k` (or `k_values`) prevents vectorization of that precomputation loop.  
- The gather/scatter pattern in the final accumulation (`flat_2d_array[k_values[j]]`) is not recognized as safe for vectorization due to possible aliasing.  

**Optimization opportunity**:  
- The original loop’s `k` update is also a recurrence, but the accumulation is directly done in the same loop, making it harder to vectorize.  
- The optimized version splits the loop but still has the same underlying indirect access pattern.  
- To enable vectorization, we could try:  
  - Using `#pragma omp simd` with `safelen(256)` to assert no repeated indices within the loop.

void s141_opt(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int outer_loop_count = 200 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_loop_count; nl++) {
        // Process each column i independently
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            // Compute initial k for this i
            int k = (i+1) * i / 2 + i;
            
            // Load bb values for this column into a contiguous array
            float bb_col[256];
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                bb_col[j] = bb[j][i];
            }
            
            // Split the loop: first compute all k values
            int k_values[256];
            int current_k = k;
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                k_values[j] = current_k;
                current_k += j + 1;
            }
            
            // Then perform accumulation with predictable access pattern
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                int idx = k_values[j];
                float temp = flat_2d_array[idx] + bb_col[j];
                flat_2d_array[idx] = temp;

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

### Original loop logic:

The `k` index jumps by `j+1` each iteration, so `k` depends on `j` in a non-linear way.

### Optimized code changes:
- It precomputes `bb_vals[j-i] = bb[j][i]` in a separate loop.
- Then in the second loop, it does `flat_2d_array[k] += bb_vals[j-i]` with same `k` update.

This **does not** change the semantics, because `bb[j][i]` is read-only in the inner loop, and `flat_2d_array[k]` is updated in the same order with the same values.  
The `bb_vals` array is just a temporary copy.

**But** — there’s a subtlety: the original `k` formula in the optimized code is written as `(i+1) * i / 2 + i`, which matches the original’s simplified form.

Compiler feedback says:
- First inner loop (`for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) { bb_vals[j - i] = bb[j][i]; }`) is not vectorized because “cost-model indicates vectorization is not beneficial” — but it’s still interleaved.
- Second inner loop (`for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) { flat_2d_array[k] += bb_vals[j - i]; k += j + 1; }`) is **not vectorized** because:
  1. `k` changes non-linearly (`k += j + 1`), so `flat_2d_array[k]` accesses are **non-contiguous** and have indirect addressing.
  2. There’s an “unsafe indirect dependence” because `k` depends on `j`, and the same memory location might be accessed in ways the compiler can’t prove as independent across iterations.

This is a **true loop-carried dependency** in the sense that the array indices are data-dependent (not a simple stride-1), so vectorization fails.

For fixed `i`, each `k` is unique (since `k` increases with `j`), so within one `i`-loop, each `flat_2d_array` element is updated at most once. That means the inner loop over `j` has no loop-carried dependency on `flat_2d_array`. The only problem is the **non-unit stride** in the store.

Non-unit stride can be vectorized with **gather** for loads and **scatter** for stores if the hardware supports it (AVX2/AVX-512). The compiler might not do it automatically due to cost model.

But the compiler feedback says “unsafe dependent memory operations” — this might be because the compiler cannot prove that `k` values don’t overlap across different `i` loops. Actually, they might overlap:

So no overlap within same `i`, but across different `i` in same `nl` iteration, the same `flat_2d_array` element might be updated multiple times (true dependency across `i` loops). That’s fine for vectorizing the inner `j` loop, but the compiler might be conservative.

Given the feedback, the current optimized code is **not vectorized**, so there is **optimization opportunity** (e.g., manually using SIMD intrinsics for the irregular store, or changing data layout).

**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops

    // Original

        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;

```c
void s141_opt(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int outer_loop_count = 200 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_loop_count; nl++) {
        // Loop distribution: separate index computation from accumulation
        // Precompute k values for all (i,j) pairs
        int k_vals[256][256];
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            int k = (i+1) * i / 2 + i;
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                k_vals[i][j] = k;
                k += j + 1;
            }
        }
        
        // Loop reordering: gather all bb values first with stride-1 access
        float bb_gathered[256][256];
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            // bb[j][i] has stride-1 in j dimension
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                bb_gathered[i][j] = bb[j][i];
            }
        }
        
        // Final accumulation loop with precomputed indices
        // This separates the indirect addressing from the computation
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                int idx = k_vals[i][j];
                flat_2d_array[idx] += bb_gathered[i][j];
            }
        }
    }

## Analysis Report

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code
The optimized code compiles successfully (no compilation errors reported). The compiler feedback shows remarks about vectorization analysis, not compilation errors.

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Source Code

**Critical Issue Found**: The optimized code has incorrect semantics.

**Problem Analysis**:
The original code computes `k` as:
```c
k = (i+1) * ((i+1) - 1) / 2 + (i+1)-1;
```
Which simplifies to:
```c
k = (i+1) * i / 2 + i;
```

However, the optimized code introduces two major semantic issues:

1. **Memory Overhead**: Creates two 256×256 arrays (`k_vals` and `bb_gathered`) totaling ~512KB, which are reallocated in every outer loop iteration.

2. **Incorrect Indexing Pattern**: The original code uses a triangular indexing pattern where `k` increments by `j+1` in the inner loop. The optimized code attempts to precompute these indices but then uses them in a way that changes the access pattern.

3. **Data Dependency Violation**: The original code has a true dependency where `flat_2d_array[k]` is read and written in the same statement. The optimized code separates this into different loops, but the precomputed `k_vals` may have overlapping indices for different `(i,j)` pairs, causing race conditions when parallelized.

**Step-by-Step Example Verification**:
For `i=0, j=0`:
- Original: `k = (0+1)*0/2 + 0 = 0`
- Optimized: Same

For `i=0, j=1`:
- Original: `k += j+1 = 0 + 1+1 = 2`
- Optimized: Should be 2

For `i=1, j=1`:
- Original: `k = (1+1)*1/2 + 1 = 2*1/2 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2`
- Optimized: Should be 2

**Conflict**: Both `(0,1)` and `(1,1)` map to index 2 in the original code. The optimized code's separate accumulation loops would have undefined behavior due to this overlapping access pattern.

**Conclusion**: The optimized code changes the semantics because it doesn't properly handle the overlapping index pattern in the original code. The original code has a complex triangular indexing scheme where multiple `(i,j)` pairs can map to the same `k` index, and the optimized code's loop distribution breaks this dependency.

**UNIT TEST**: The unit test indicates "UNIT TEST PASS," but based on semantic analysis, the optimized code has different behavior due to the overlapping index issue.

Since the semantics are incorrect, we cannot proceed to vectorization analysis. The optimized code needs to be fixed first.

The optimized code has incorrect semantics due to:
1. Failure to handle overlapping indices in the original triangular access pattern
2. Introduction of unnecessary memory overhead
3. Breaking of true dependencies in the original computation

The code needs to be rewritten to preserve the original semantics while attempting optimization opportunities.

**Objective**: Generate a semantically equivalent vectorize-friendly version of `*_opt` using:  
**Preservation Requirements**:  
1. Function signature

If the original value is used, e.g. a[i+1] is used for iteration i, a[i+1] should be stored in a temporary variable(use memcpy to store orign value) or used before updating a[i+1]
If the updated value is used, e.g. a[i-1] is used for iteration i, a[i-1] should update before using it.

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.
**When to Apply**:  
    - Example:  
     ```c
    // Original
    if (cond1)  
        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

## ITERATE OUTPUT
Looking at the original code, I can see the key challenge: `flat_2d_array[k]` has a complex triangular indexing pattern where multiple `(i,j)` pairs can map to the same `k` index. This creates loop-carried dependencies that prevent vectorization. Let me reorganize the loops to separate the dependency while preserving semantics.

```c
void s141_opt(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int outer_loop_count = 200 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_loop_count; nl++) {
        // Loop distribution: separate the accumulation into independent chunks
        // We'll process each unique k value separately
        
        // First, compute all contributions for each (i,j) pair
        float contributions[256][256];
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                contributions[i][j] = bb[j][i];
            }
        }
        
        // Now accumulate contributions for each unique k
        // We need to find all (i,j) pairs that map to the same k
        
        // Maximum k value occurs when i=255, j=255
        // k = (i+1)*i/2 + i + sum_{t=i}^{j-1} (t+1)
        // For i=255, j=255: k = 256*255/2 + 255 = 32895
        
        // Create temporary array to accumulate contributions for each k
        float temp_accum[32896] = {0};  // +1 for safety
        
        // Accumulate all contributions into temp array
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            int k = (i+1) * i / 2 + i;
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                temp_accum[k] += contributions[i][j];
                k += j + 1;
            }
        }
        
        // Now add accumulated values to flat_2d_array
        // This separates the dependency from the accumulation
        for (int idx = 0; idx < 32896; idx++) {
            if (temp_accum[idx] != 0.0f) {
                flat_2d_array[idx] += temp_accum[idx];
            }
        }
    }
}

## **2. Semantic Comparison**

The original code:

- Outer loop runs `200 * (iterations / 256)` times.
- For each `i` from 0 to 255:
  - `k` is initialized as `(i+1)*i/2 + i` (since `(i+1)*((i+1)-1)/2 + (i+1)-1` simplifies to `(i+1)*i/2 + i`).
  - For each `j` from `i` to 255:
    - `flat_2d_array[k] += bb[j][i]`
    - `k += j+1`

This means each `(i,j)` pair maps to a unique `k` (it’s essentially mapping a triangular region of `bb` into a 1D array using a variable stride).

The optimized code:

- Creates `contributions[256][256]` and copies `bb[j][i]` into it for `j >= i`.
- Then uses a temporary array `temp_accum[32896]` to accumulate contributions for each `k`.
- Finally adds `temp_accum` to `flat_2d_array`.

{
    int k;

Optimized code:
```c

void s141_opt(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int outer_loop_count = 200 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_loop_count; nl++) {
        // Loop distribution: separate the accumulation into independent chunks
        // We'll process each unique k value separately
        
        // First, compute all contributions for each (i,j) pair
        float contributions[256][256];
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                contributions[i][j] = bb[j][i];
            }
        }
        
        // Now accumulate contributions for each unique k
        // We need to find all (i,j) pairs that map to the same k
        
        // Maximum k value occurs when i=255, j=255
        // k = (i+1)*i/2 + i + sum_{t=i}^{j-1} (t+1)
        // For i=255, j=255: k = 256*255/2 + 255 = 32895
        
        // Create temporary array to accumulate contributions for each k
        float temp_accum[32896] = {0};  // +1 for safety
        
        // Accumulate all contributions into temp array
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            int k = (i+1) * i / 2 + i;
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                temp_accum[k] += contributions[i][j];
                k += j + 1;
            }
        }
        
        // Now add accumulated values to flat_2d_array
        // This separates the dependency from the accumulation
        for (int idx = 0; idx < 32896; idx++) {
            if (temp_accum[idx] != 0.0f) {
                flat_2d_array[idx] += temp_accum[idx];
            }
        }
    }
}

```

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:
Use temp variable to store result to avoid complex memory access

Replace multi-way conditionals with arithmetic masking, ensuring uniform execution paths across iterations.

    // Original

        x = value_A;  
    else if (cond2)  
        x = value_B;  
    else  
        x = value_C;
    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;  
     ```

## Analysis

### 1. Compilation Errors in Optimized Code
The optimized code compiles successfully (no syntax errors). However, there are significant semantic issues.

### 2. Semantic Comparison with Simple Test Cases

**Key Issues Identified:**
1. **Memory allocation errors**: The optimized code declares large arrays `k_values[256][256]` and `contributions[256][256]` on the stack inside the outer loop. This allocates 256×256×2 arrays = 131,072 integers + 131,072 floats ≈ 1MB total, which may cause stack overflow and is inefficient.

2. **Incorrect k calculation**: The original formula is `k = (i+1) * ((i+1) - 1) / 2 + (i+1)-1`, which simplifies to `(i+1)*i/2 + i`. The optimized code uses this simplified form, which is correct.

3. **Semantic difference**: The original code has a **true dependency** where `k` is updated within the inner loop (`k += j+1`), and this updated `k` is used in the next iteration of the `j` loop. The optimized code attempts to separate this by precomputing all `k` values, but this changes the memory access pattern and introduces unnecessary storage.

4. **Unit test failure**: The unit test indicates inconsistent output, confirming the semantic difference.

**Step-by-step example verification:**
For `i=0, j=0`:
- Original: `k = (0+1)*0/2 + 0 = 0`, accesses `flat_2d_array[0] += bb[0][0]`, then `k += 0+1 = 1`
- Optimized: Precomputes `k=0`, accesses `flat_2d_array[0] += bb[0][0]` (same)

For `i=0, j=1`:
- Original: Uses `k=1` from previous iteration, accesses `flat_2d_array[1] += bb[1][0]`
- Optimized: Precomputes `k=1`, accesses `flat_2d_array[1] += bb[1][0]` (same)

While the access pattern appears the same, the unit test failure suggests there's an issue with the large stack allocations or iteration counts.

**Root cause**: The optimized code introduces unnecessary memory overhead and doesn't actually improve vectorization. The compiler feedback shows the second loop still has unsafe indirect dependencies because `flat_2d_array[k]` accesses are irregular and dependent on precomputed indices.

### 3. Vectorization Analysis

**Compiler feedback analysis:**
1. Loop not vectorized due to "unsafe dependent memory operations" - the irregular access to `flat_2d_array[k]` prevents vectorization.
2. The precomputation approach doesn't help because the fundamental issue remains: `flat_2d_array[k]` has indirect addressing that varies with `j`.

**Why vectorization fails:**
- The original loop has an **irregular memory access pattern**: `flat_2d_array[k]` where `k` changes non-linearly with `j`.
- This creates unpredictable memory dependencies that the compiler cannot analyze for vectorization safety.
- The optimized code doesn't resolve this fundamental issue.

**Optimization opportunities missed:**
1. **Loop distribution not applied**: The compiler suggests using `#pragma clang loop distribute(enable)` but this wasn't implemented.
2. **No actual vectorization improvements**: The code still has the same irregular access pattern.
3. **Unnecessary memory overhead**: Precomputing indices and values wastes memory and doesn't help vectorization.

### Conclusion: FAIL

void s141(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int k;
    for (int nl = 0; nl < 200*(iterations/256); nl++) {
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            k = (i+1) * ((i+1) - 1) / 2 + (i+1)-1;
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                flat_2d_array[k] += bb[j][i];
                k += j+1;
            }
        }
    }
}
```

Optimized code:
```c

void s141_opt(int iterations, float * flat_2d_array, float bb[256][256])
{
    int outer_loop_count = 200 * (iterations / 256);
    
    for (int nl = 0; nl < outer_loop_count; nl++) {
        // Precompute all k values and contributions first
        int k_values[256][256];
        float contributions[256][256];
        
        // First loop: compute all k values and contributions (no dependencies)
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            int k = (i+1) * i / 2 + i;  // Simplified from original formula
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                k_values[i][j] = k;
                contributions[i][j] = bb[j][i];
                k += j + 1;
            }
        }
        
        // Second loop: accumulate contributions using precomputed k values
        // This separates the irregular memory access from the computation
        for (int i = 0; i < 256; i++) {
            for (int j = i; j < 256; j++) {
                int k = k_values[i][j];
                flat_2d_array[k] += contributions[i][j];
            }
        }
    }
}

**Possible Transformations**  
Loop Splitting:  
**Type 1 - Instruction Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Multiple independent statements exist within the loop body  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i) { 
       A[i] = ...;  // Independent group 1
       B[i] = ...;  // Independent group 2 
     }
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 1  
   for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  // Split loop 2  
   ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Total iteration count remains identical  
   - Memory access order preserved across all arrays  

**Type 2 - Iteration Range Splitting**  
   *When to use*:  
   - Loop contains phase-dependent patterns (e.g., mid-point update)  
   - Vectorization blocked by conditional value changes  
   - Example pattern:  
     ```c
     for(i=0; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
     ```  
   *Implementation*:  
   ```c
   // Phase 1: i=0~mid (pre-update)  
   for(i=0; i<mid; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   // Mid-point update  
   a[mid] = a[mid] + b[mid];  
   // Phase 2: i=mid+1~end (post-update)  
   for(i=mid+1; i<LEN; i++) a[i] = a[mid] + b[i];  
   ```  
   *Validation Checklist*:  
   - Split boundaries cover original iteration range  
   - No overlapping writes between split segments  

Loop Reordering and Instruction Reordering:
**Application Rules**:  
   *Priority Patterns*:  
   - Move loop-invariant computations upward  
     ```c
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = x + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = x + y;
     ```  
   - Solve loop dependency
    ```c
    // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = y * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = y * z;  // y used earlier
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     // Before reorder:
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;  // y is invariant
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     
     // After reorder:
     b[i] = a[i+1] * z;
     a[i] = b[i-1] + y;
     ```
   - Cluster memory operations with similar addresses 

Loop Distribution:
**When to Apply**:  
   - Multiple independent write targets in loop body  
   - Example:  
     ```c
     // Original
     for(i) {
       A[i] = ...;  // Writes to A
       B[i] = ...;  // Writes to B (independent)
     }
     
     // Distributed
     for(i) { A[i] = ...; }  
     for(i) { B[i] = ...; }  
     ```  
   *Validation*:  
   - Distributed loops must have identical iteration ranges  
   - No interleaved access between distributed loops  

Use temporary variable:

    else

    // Optimized  
    x = cond1 * value_A + (cond2 && !cond1) * value_B + (!cond1 && !cond2) * value_C;
